Praxis Precision Medicines, Inc.: Manager CMC Materials Management

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

At Praxis, every material movement matters. We are seeking a proactive, results-driven Manager, CMC Materials Management to own domestic and international logistics supporting our research, development, and manufacturing programs.

This role ensures critical materials reach the right destination, on time and in the required condition, while maintaining compliance, inventory visibility, and cost efficiency across our global supplier and CDMO network. The ideal candidate anticipates challenges, solves problems with urgency, and takes ownership from planning through delivery. Success requires strong execution, attention to detail, and a commitment to advancing life-altering medicines for patients.

The physical and mental requirements of our roles include but are not limited to regular use of a computer, devices or other office equipment, clear communication, and occasional movement.  You'll need comfort with screen work, basic hand coordination, and focus.  Reasonable accommodations may be made to enable individuals with disabilities to perform these functions.

We provide annualized base salary compensation in the range listed below. Final salary range may be modified commensurate with job level, education, and experience.

Praxis Precision Medicines is a clinical-stage biopharmaceutical company translating genetic insights into the development of therapies for central nervous system disorders characterized by neuronal imbalance.  At Praxis we share a common vision of reshaping the human condition into a more freeing and fulfilled existence by developing high impact medicines for patients and families affected by and living with complex brain disorders.  Our core Values of Trust, Ownership, Curiosity and Results are foundational to every aspect of our business and are exemplified by each and every one of our team members.Diversity, Equity & InclusionGuided by our core values, at Praxis Precision Medicines, Inc. we continue to DARE FOR MORE® to advance, promote, and champion diversity, equity, and inclusion by encouraging individuals to bring their authentic selves and perspectives to work each day. ‘Harry Potter’s Best Spin-Off Is Officially a Streaming Hit Before HBO’s Reboot

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Fifteen years after the final Harry Potter movie, and just over a month before HBO's reboot arrives on December 25, the world still has room for more magic. Harry Potter: Wizards of Baking has become a fan-favorite addition to the Wizarding World of Harry Potter, bringing the beloved franchise back to screens with a baking competition on Food Network.

The show is graced with guest judges who are actors and actresses from the movie franchise that any fan will recognize immediately. The beloved story is a draw for the audience and the bakers, who all take great care in producing the most spectacular pieces of art out of pastries and candy. It is clear how much thought each baker puts into their creations and the flavors they bring to the table. Harry Potter: Wizards of Baking is a charming show that has brought magic to Food Network in a way that fans will surely enjoy.

Harry Potter: Wizards of Baking focuses on baking while weaving in the Harry Potter franchise audiences know and love. The show is hosted by not one, but two actors from the Harry Potter films, James Phelps and Oliver Phelps, who play the Weasley twins in all eight movies. The first season introduces nine teams, sent through the fireplace via the flue network, who are paired together based on their shared love for one specific part of Harry Potter. The teams get to work immediately with their first task: a two-foot-tall sculpture of a scene or set piece from the film. Ultimately, only six teams got on the Hogwarts Express and advanced to the next episode.

Even with baking being such a large part of the show, it is centered around the Wizarding World. James and Oliver Phelps do a marvelous job of hosting with funny quips and references. Notably, the Phelps twins are not judging the competition but get the chance to snack on the treats being made, even being given an entire tray of leftover mango caramel. In Season 1, Harry Potter: Wizards of Baking was judged by Carla Hall, a Food Network regular, and Jozef Youssef, an award-winning chef. Joining the pair of highly acclaimed chefs are celebrity cameos such as Bonnie Wright, Evanna Lynch, and Warwick Davis – who play Ginny Weasley, Luna Lovegood, and Professor Flitwick, respectively.

Four worlds and four completely different ideas of what a good life looks like. This is where you belong, what it would ask of you, and what you would get back in return.

You belong somewhere old enough that the mountains have names and somebody can still tell you who gave them. Middle-earth is for people who think the right thing is worth doing even when it will not be finished in their lifetime.

You want the sea, a crew, and absolutely nobody telling you where you are allowed to go. The Grand Line is the world for people whose main ambition in life is finding out what is over there.

You would rather be feared and useful than liked and useless. The Continent is a world where the monsters are the straightforward part of the job and the people are where it all gets complicated.

You want the myths to be real and loose in the actual world, and you want to find out at about thirteen that you were never anywhere near as ordinary as everybody kept telling you.

The guest stars are not the only thing Harry Potter fans can expect from Harry Potter: Wizards of Baking. There are references to the Harry Potter films everywhere. Harry Potter: Wizards of Baking is shot at Warner Bros. Studios and has pieces from the film, from the Hogwarts Express to a portrait of the Fat Lady that hangs over the fireplace. With clips of the films cut in between each reference to remind the audience of what some scenes looked like, and each challenge being something wonderful and beloved from the film, there is no forgetting that Harry Potter: Wizards of Baking is for the fans.

The series is now in its third season, which premiered November 4 with a Hogwarts House tournament and new judges Duff Goldman and Sarah Mountain. Per FlixPatrol for the Week of November 9, Wizards of Baking ranked No. 5 on HBO Max's U.S. Top 10 TV Shows chart, giving fans another way to revisit the Wizarding World before the December reboot.

From the beginning, it is clear that this is a baking show, so although knowledge and love of the Harry Potter franchise are massive benefits to the bakers, the taste and creativity will move them further in the competition. The nine baking teams get to show off their knowledge of Harry Potter and baking skills with themed challenges. Moving parts are in many of the pieces, including lights, chocolate fountains, spiders on tracks, and messages hidden in clear candy.

Each team can show off two tasting portions, one for each baker (several of whom appeared on Is It Cake? on Netflix). The teams fight to keep their spot in the competition and learn to work with each other in the kitchen. The show's charm is the baking and the teams' apparent care when creating each piece.

The magic and beauty of Hogwarts have come to Food Network with Harry Potter: Wizards of Baking, and it does not disappoint. Harry Potter: Wizards of Baking is delightful, with James and Oliver Phelps flourishing as hosts and joined by their co-stars. The baking is also spectacular, and it is evident from the intricacies and detail how much care goes into crafting each confection. Incorporating one of the most beloved franchises with a cooking competition was an excellent move for Food Network and has produced a fun and charming show that any fan will love. If food and fandom are what you're looking for, Harry Potter: Wizards of Baking takes the cake.

Harry Potter: Wizards of Baking airs weekly on Food Network and can be streamed on HBO Max with a subscription.

Stayd Collective: Cleaning Operations Coordinator

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Schedule: Monday–Friday, 8:00am–5:00pm ET | Occasional weekend coverage as needed

Compensation: USD $1,200–$1,500 / month depending on experience.

About the brandHaven manages cabins and mountain homes in the Great Smoky Mountains and around Knoxville, Tennessee. Steep driveways, hot tubs, wood stoves, and a season that swings from quiet weeks to a full house every weekend. It is a market where property care is guest experience: a cold hot tub is a ruined anniversary, and a turnover that runs late is a family waiting in the driveway.

The roleYou are the person who makes sure every cabin is guest-ready before the next family arrives. You are the main communication point between Haven operations and our Tendwell cleaning teams. Your day starts with the departures, arrivals and cleaning schedule, and from there you follow up with cleaners on progress and ETAs, clear whatever is blocking a turn, coordinate re-cleans and supply fixes, and confirm each home is ready before a same-day check-in.

When a guest checks out late, you work out what it does to the cleaning schedule and tell operations what timing is realistic. It is a role for someone who is highly responsive and calm under same-day pressure. Asking for an update is not the same as getting the home ready. If a turn is yours, you own it until it is confirmed complete.

Where you sitYou sit in Housekeeping & Field Ops and work every day with Tendwell cleaners and Haven’s operations team. This is not a role where you are handed a queue and left alone.

This role is remote and based in LATAM, covering U.S. Eastern Time working hours. Because Stayd runs shared systems across every brand, a process you improve here tends to end up running in six other markets, which is the closest thing we have to a promotion path.

Apply here in about four minutes on a phone. There are a handful of questions written to find out what you can actually do, a resume, and a short voice introduction for roles where spoken English is part of the work. There is no cover letter and there is no portal account to create.

You can listen to your recording and try again as many times as you need. If a microphone, privacy, or accessibility issue prevents recording, request another screening option and a recruiter will see it.

Every single applicant hears back from us, including a no. If we are not moving forward we will tell you, and we will tell you why. If you need an accommodation to complete this application, email [email protected]. Asking never counts against you.

By applying, you agree that Stayd may contact you about your application by email and WhatsApp. Reply STOP at any time to opt out.

Scotland’s waterlogged landscape hides thousands of years of carbon

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

A satellite image of Scotland’s Flow Country reveals a waterlogged landscape where peat bogs have stored carbon for thousands of years.

Today’s Image of the Day from NASA Earth Observatory features the Flow Country, a peatland landscape in the Scottish Highlands.

Landsat 8’s Operational Land Imager captured this view on March 4, 2024, recording a spring scene in one of Europe’s most important blanket bog regions.

Dark brown ground stretches between scattered lakes in northern Scotland, broken by green fields and sharply outlined blocks of trees.

From above, the contrast between bogs, farmland, and tree plantations becomes clear.

What the image cannot show is the deep store of ancient plant material beneath the bogs, where carbon has been accumulating for thousands of years.

The brown expanses mark boggy ground dotted with lakes.

Around Thurso, on the right side of the image, lighter green farmland contrasts with the darker green shapes of conifer plantations closer to the center.

Those geometric tree plantations stand out against the irregular outlines of water and peatland.

Together, the patterns show how farming and forestry occupy parts of a region whose distinctive character comes from persistent wetness.

At ground level, the bog’s surface has a texture that the satellite view cannot fully resolve.

Mossy hummocks rise above hollows, while pools interrupt the vegetation. Grasses and heathers grow across this uneven, largely treeless terrain.

“Blanket bog” describes how peat spreads over the landscape, covering slopes and hollows rather than remaining confined to a single basin.

Its formation requires conditions that keep the ground wet for long periods.

Northern Scotland has a useful combination of climate and terrain for building bogs.

Rain arrives frequently from the Atlantic, while rocks, soils, and landforms slow its escape.

Cool conditions also limit water loss through evaporation and plants.

Under these circumstances, water collects faster than the landscape can shed it. Bog plants grow, die, and leave their remains in saturated ground.

Usually, microbes break down dead vegetation, returning much of its carbon to the atmosphere.

Waterlogged soils restrict oxygen, slowing that process. Some plant material remains only partly decomposed and gradually becomes peat.

The growing peat then helps retain more water, sustaining conditions in which additional bog plants can flourish.

Over time, this relationship between vegetation and water builds a thick organic layer beneath the living surface.

Sphagnum mosses play a central role in that process. Their contribution is easy to overlook beside a forest’s towering trees, yet these small plants help create an ecosystem capable of preserving carbon far beyond a single growing season.

Parts of the Flow Country have been forming peat for around 9,000 years.

In places, deposits exceed eight meters in thickness, preserving the accumulated remains of countless generations of plants.

That depth also holds evidence of environmental change.

Pollen and plant fossils within the peat record earlier vegetation, offering researchers a way to investigate how the landscape developed and how people influenced it.

In 2024, the Flow Country joined UNESCO’s World Heritage List.

The designated property covers approximately 1,870 square kilometers across seven component areas, recognizing an exceptional example of an actively accumulating blanket bog.

Its value extends beyond what lies underground. The mixture of pools, ridges, wet hollows, and vegetation supports distinctive wildlife communities, including breeding birds.

More than 30 species of sphagnum moss occur here, adding variety to ground that can appear uniform from a distance.

The Flow Country illustrates a much larger global pattern.

Peatlands occupy only about 3% of Earth’s land surface, yet the United Nations Environment Programme reports that they store roughly twice as much carbon as all the world’s forests.

That comparison concerns carbon already stored, rather than the amount absorbed each year.

Much of a peatland’s importance comes from protecting material that accumulated long before modern climate records began.

Drainage can disrupt that protection. As peat dries and oxygen enters, decomposition accelerates, allowing stored carbon to escape as carbon dioxide. Burning can also release carbon accumulated over centuries.

UNESCO identifies historical drainage and woodland creation among the pressures that have affected parts of the Flow Country.

Restoration is therefore part of protecting the landscape’s future. Seen from orbit, the bogs offer few obvious clues to their age or depth.

The brown surface is simply the visible top of a much older system, still growing wherever water allows plant remains to accumulate.

Image Credit: Michala Garrison, NASA Earth Observatory, using Landsat data from the U.S. Geological Survey.

Carolyn Murphy, a Lifelong Minimalist, Embraces Maximalism with Her La DoubleJ Collaboration

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

It’s impossible not to feel a rush of joy the moment you step into an evening hosted by La DoubleJ. And on Tuesday night, that infectious energy permeated a private dining room at the Beverly Hills Hotel, where the Milan-based label threw a dinner party celebrating its new collaboration with supermodel Carolyn Murphy. “It’s relaxing to have fun with your guests—otherwise, it’s no bueno,” the label’s founder, J.J. Martin, told Vogue. “You have to raise the vibration yourself and exude that energy around the room.”

The storied venue was a sentimental location choice for Martin, who grew up in the Pacific Palisades. “My family is from Los Angeles, so this hotel is just like a classic center of gravity for the city, and also a great point between East meets West,” she explained, adding that her grandfather lived in Beverly Hills.

It felt as if the founder had invited a large group of friends—Liv Tyler, Abby Elliott, Jackie Tohn, Sepideh Moafi, and Natasha Rothwell among them—to her splashy, eclectic Milan home. The tables were a feast for the eyes, adorned with multicolored flowers and bright dinnerware, and dressed in a floral tablecloth.

Lela Becker, co-founder and president of Mother, introduced Martin and Murphy at one of Mother’s events around the time of the tragic Los Angeles wildfires, which had destroyed the model’s home. “Lela was like, ‘You’ve got to get out of your hotel,’” Murphy recalled. As it turned out, Martin’s warmth and uplifting spirit were exactly what she needed. “When J.J. and I met at the event, it was like fireworks went off right away.” After the two got to know each other better, Martin floated the idea of a collaboration.

“But I don’t wear prints,” Murphy recalled telling Martin with a laugh. Indeed, the model is a longtime minimalist, but even she couldn’t pass up the opportunity to enter the lively, colorful world of La DoubleJ. “I’m pushing myself out of my comfort zone, and I think that’s why the collaboration was a lovely marriage of our sensibilities,” Murphy explained. Fittingly, she was clad in a champagne-hued two-piece set with bezel-set stones from the collection, which is named Sisterhood of Light.

“My daughter, Dylan, is standing here in the Camelot dress,” Murphy said, pointing out the 25-year-old, who was socializing behind her. Meanwhile, Moafi, who stars in season 2 of The Pitt, was in awe of the collaboration. “It is so diverse and a cross blend of so many different cultures and time periods,” the actor, dressed in a 1970s-inspired patterned blouse and matching trousers, said.

On the outdoor patio, where guests chatted and sipped on cocktails before dinner, Rothwell, known for her role in The White Lotus, looked as though she had just stepped off the set in Thailand, wearing a flowing green-and-teal printed dress. “My stylist Katie Bottomler is the one who introduced me to the brand while I was doing press for The White Lotus,” she shared, with Bottomler standing by her side. Luckily, the label’s airy designs helped Rothwell beat the heat while in Thailand, too. “I’m a plus-size girly, and so I love a brand that considers that and looks good on all body types,” Rothwell added.

After admiring the aforementioned tablescapes and snapping photos, attendees began finding their seats, where copies of Martin’s book Table Energy: Entertaining with Italian Spirit awaited. “By the way, product plug, everything on your table has been made by La DoubleJ,” Martin told the crowd, prompting laughter and applause as they enjoyed artichoke salad, grilled branzino, and filet mignon.

J.J. Martin, Carolyn Murphy and Liv Tyler

Natalia Bonifacci, Lizzy Mathis and Djuna Bel

'Yes': SBI CEO Yoshitaka Kitao Answers XRP Holder Doubts in One Word

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Yoshitaka Kitao, head of Japanese financial group SBI Holdings, answered "YES" when asked point-blank whether he still trusts XRP.

The question came from an X user. The user wrote that the past few weeks had been "truly exhausting" for XRP holders, and said that token holders feel that Ripple has left them behind.

Kitao answered in English. Most of his recent posts on X are in Japanese, and the rest of them are reposts of other accounts.

Kitao previously worked as an executive at Nomura Securities and later served as CFO of SoftBank. In 1999, he founded SoftBank Investment, which later became SBI Holdings. Today, SBI includes banks, brokerage, and insurance businesses, as well as crypto services.

The partnership began in 2016. SBI joined Ripple's Series B round and launched a joint venture with Ripple, SBI Ripple Asia. SBI owns about 60% of the venture, and Ripple owns the remaining 40%.

The same year, SBI Ripple Asia set up a consortium of Japanese banks to test Ripple's technology for payments, and the consortium later grew to more than 60 banks.

In 2018, SBI launched the interbank app MoneyTap. The same year, SBI's crypto exchange, now called SBI VC Trade, started operations with XRP trading. In 2019, Kitao joined Ripple's board of directors.

From 2020, SBI began handing the token out to shareholders as bonuses, and the payment service SBI Remit launched remittances using it with on-demand liquidity (ODL), including in the major Japan-Philippines corridor.

SBI kept working with Ripple during the SEC lawsuit against the company, which lasted from December 2020 until 2025.

Back on February 15, 2026, in rare long-form tweets in English, Kitao denied rumors that SBI holds $10 billion worth of XRP: "Not $10 billion in XRP, but around 9% of Ripple Labs. So our hidden asset could be much bigger." By his account, the combined valuation of Ripple Labs and its ecosystem is "enormous," and SBI owns more than 9% of all of it.

Kitao also talked about XRP at SBI's quarterly briefing on November 2, 2026. He said that investors buy Bitcoin mostly to hold it, and that the value of XRP depends on its use in payments and business ecosystems.

In August 2025, SBI VC Trade and Ripple agreed to bring the RLUSD stablecoin to Japan. In October 2025, SBI also invested $200 million in Evernorth, a company that holds XRP in its treasury. By now, SBI VC Trade has more than two million accounts.

CJ McCollum joins NBA on Prime crew, discusses role on Hawks, Damian Lillard's comeback

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

CJ McCollum hit the game-winning shot in the Atlanta Hawks' Game 3 victory over the New York Knicks in the first round of last year's playoffs.

Not only does he remember that shot, but he remembers enjoying being a guest on Prime Video's "NBA Nightcap" postgame show where he appeared in his pajamas and the hosts joked he put the Knicks "to sleep." They all laughed about his role of villain as his son popped in to say hello.

The party doesn't have to stop there as NBA on Prime announced on Thursday, Nov. 12 that the 14-year veteran guard will be a player commentator for several programs this season starting with "NBA Nightcap" on Friday, Nov. 13.

"The conversation and team analysis that night was really fun," he told USA TODAY Sports of the postgame memories, "and I thought the chemistry was there, and I felt like it made a lot of sense (for me to join the Prime team). And I have a great appreciation for what NBA on Prime is doing, what the crew is doing, and how they educate and share authentic analysis."

McCollum, 35, majored in journalism at Lehigh University and has experience hosting his own podcast with "The CJ McCollum Show" and "Pull Up with CJ McCollum."

Among Prime's All-Star team of talent that he is joining is a mix of longtime broadcasters, including Kevin Harlan, and basketball greats like Udonis Haslem, Dirk Nowitzki, Steve Nash and Candace Parker. McCollum said the team is "a really cool atmosphere, really great environment of people that I respect and admire."

"They're very honest. They watch the game. They're students of the game. They're people who appreciate the game and they do a great job of describing why certain things are happening," he continued. "... When you have that group of professionals who have played the game, have talked the game, have lived the game, have been around it and seen it for so long, you get various impactful moments where viewers are able to really get a glimpse of what's actually happening, why it's happening, in an insightful way that they can digest."

McCollum appreciates the craft of journalism and how showing the human element of any story brings people together. As journalism continues to face industry challenges, the 2016 Most Improved Player has hope.

"Storytelling is vital. I think it makes the world a better place," he said. "Being able to hear people's stories, being able to see people's journeys not just in the sports world but in life, I think is something that we all can appreciate.

"And journalism isn't dying. It's just evolving. But I think the people who move with integrity, who do things the right way and really appreciate the craft are continuing to thrive for sure."

McCollum shared with USA TODAY Sports some thoughts about various NBA topics as a sneak peek of what fans can expect from him as a guest commentator on Prime Video.

McCollum re-signed with the Hawks this summer after joining the team from the Washington Wizards last season in the Trae Young trade. He remains a veteran leader even as he is coming off the bench and reached the 17,000 points mark when he scored 21 in Atlanta's loss on Monday, Nov. 9 to the Los Angeles Lakers.

"It means a lot," he said of reaching the scoring mark. "It's a sign of longevity, being able to be around for a long time and play at a high level. It's a sign of hard work and appreciation for the sport. But it's also a sign of playing with good teammates, good coaching staff, people that have been able to help me get the best out of my talent and utilize a lot of the resources that we have around us and turn that into some success on the court."

Jalen Johnson is a rising star for Atlanta who helped the Hawks get to a four-game winning streak before the loss. On Saturday, Nov. 7, the 24-year-old scored 30 points in his return from an ankle injury to help with a comeback win over the Brooklyn Nets.

"Obviously, the scoring speaks for itself," McCollum said. "His vision, I think, could be one of his best traits. He's a great passer, leading transition, elite on the break, great paint finisher, and a guy who rebounds at a high level as well. And I think he's a guy that really appreciates the game, is a student of the game, studies it, lives it, and I think the sky is the limit for him."

Atlanta was on full display last month when the Atlanta Dream made it to the WNBA Finals. Angel Reese and company lost the championship to the Golden State Valkyries, but made headlines with performances from Quavo, Big Boi and a heartwarming national anthem from T.I.'s daughter, Heiress Harris.

McCollum pointed out that, at the time of the interview, the Usher and Chris Brown tour was in town and the Atlanta Falcons were in the middle of the NFL season as extra reason that the city was the place to be.

"The energy is always high in Atlanta," he said. "... Atlanta's in a really great place. It's obviously a place of its own, where you have food, you have culture, you have people from different walks of life."

Something this season that NBA fans were anticipating was the return of Damian Lillard. McCollum was teammates with the nine-time All-Star on the Portland Trail Blazers, where they both started their careers. McCollum was there for eight seasons before being traded to the New Orleans Pelicans midway though the 2021-22 season.

Lillard tore his Achilles tendon in the 2025 playoffs with the Milwaukee Bucks and missed the entirety of last season after going back to Portland. In his comeback this fall, he's averaging 20 points per game, had two 25-point efforts and his jumpshot still looks like water.

"I think there was uncertainty from the outside. But for people that knew Dame, know his work ethic, his diligence, his approach to things, we knew he was going to have this type of comeback," he said. "He's worked extremely hard, and I'm thankful and happy to be his friend and have played with him for so long. And to see him be able to overcome something like this is not surprising at all.

"I've seen how he's worked. (We've) been in contact with each other consistently. That's my guy, spent some time together in Oregon when I was out there in the summer, and this is a guy who takes his craft very seriously. He's a pro's pro, takes care of his body, takes care of his mind, and he's in a great space being home again and being back in Oregon, where it all started, that I think is a match made in heaven for him, and I'm happy to see him thriving."

Another hot topic of conversation in the NBA is the second apron, which was established in the 2023 collective bargaining agreement when McCollum was president of the NBPA. The rule creates what can be compared to a hard salary cap that has firm penalties if exceeded.

"The conception of this years ago was more about creating parity," McCollum said, "owners, teams, the union coming to an agreement that there needed to be some changes within the systems of how to strengthen some of the lower tier markets."

The second apron has been criticized for doing too much to squash superteams and breaking up championship rosters. Bruce Brown specifically said that he had trouble in free agency and teams were using the second apron as a reason not to sign him. Karl-Anthony Towns, who won the NBA Finals with the Knicks last season, also said he's had difficulties getting a contract he thinks he's worthy of.

McCollum said that only three years into the new CBA, it's too early to determine if a rule like the second apron is working.

"When you're creating something, you don't necessarily know how it's going to turn out, obviously," he continued. "And as you kind of go through something, you kind of figure out the amendments that need to take place, make sure that teams aren't being punished for drafting well.

"... We're only a few years into it. There's more things that need to be evaluated. It's hard to project what types of impacts something like an apron is going to have on the actual management and construction of a roster and how that affects contracts in the long run. But I think you need more data, more information to truly judge whether or not you're heading in the right direction."

Houston Rockets guard Fred VanVleet became NBPA president in 2025 and McCollum applauded his successor's handling of the role.

"He's doing a phenomenal job," he said. "A guy who's very mature, has continued to figure out ways to help build the business of basketball, collaborating with the NBA from a partnership standpoint. I think he's doing an excellent job. I think his leadership, obviously, on the basketball court, but his leadership off the court is something that people respect, his peers respect, and excited to see what he continues to do for not only our players but also for the league."

SoftBank’s billion-dollar loss may have put a cap on AI’s natural gas ambitions

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

SoftBank might not be the first to invest in a hot trend, but when it does, it goes big.

Witness its $30 billion bet on OpenAI in early 2025 or its announcement in February that its SB Energy subsidiary would spend $33 billion on a 9.2-gigawatt natural gas power plant. If you can’t be first, be biggest, I guess.

That philosophy apparently spilled over into procurement, where orders were also supersized. Starting about two years ago, SoftBank reportedly put down deposits on more than 13 gigawatts of turbines, almost 50% more than it needed.

The decision to over-order undoubtedly helped secure turbines and speed up SoftBank’s projects.

“SB Energy did not have a choice except to overbook turbines in the future if they wanted some turbines right away,” Peter Gardett, CEO of Noreva, told TechCrunch via email.

The energy world was caught off guard by the swiftness and magnitude of AI’s rising power demands. Queues for turbine orders now extend into the early 2030s. As natural gas was shaping up to be a go-to power source for the AI boom, CEO Masayoshi Son bet that by deploying his investment firm’s significant war chest, he could corner a portion of the market. If the AI world wanted power for its data centers, it would have to come to him.

Now, that bet appears to have fizzled. SoftBank announced a $7.1 billion quarterly loss on Tuesday, with over half of that stemming from losses related to power-generating equipment. The investor had paid turbine manufacturers nonrefundable deposits to secure orders, and now it considered much of those worthless.

The move was also a costly bit of price discovery, Gardett said. Rather than overpay and potentially still be short of turbines, SoftBank overpaid and over-ordered.

“This charge is just that price discovery finally happening,” Gardett said.

The magnitude of that misplaced bet — over $3 billion — reflects some of the electricity mania that has overtaken the AI world. Faced with years-long waits to connect to the grid, many hyperscalers and developers have turned to natural gas as a sort of energy security blanket, something that is proven and familiar.

As AI data centers spend more time in operation, their operators are getting a better feel for what’s required to keep them operating. Natural gas might seem like a safe bet, but there are still risks, including winter curtailments when supplies dip and demand spikes, Brent Nelson, senior managing director of markets and strategy at Ascend Analytics, told the Equity podcast Wednesday.

In light of that, Nelson said the grid might still be their best bet. Most data centers would be best served by a grid connection, albeit a smaller one than they might normally request. To make up for the shortfall, large batteries are a cost-effective choice, Nelson said. They’d help shave peak loads or ride out requests from the utility to curtail electricity use.

Data center operators might be coming to that same conclusion, leading the natural gas market to develop more slowly than SoftBank expected.

SoftBank’s natural gas-related losses are unlikely to have a domino effect, leading to mass cancellations of gas power plants. AI’s power demand appears to be too large for that. For now, natural gas is riding high, though SoftBank may have found its ceiling.

De Chant is also a lecturer in MIT’s Graduate Program in Science Writing, and he was awarded a Knight Science Journalism Fellowship at MIT in 2018, during which time he studied climate technologies and explored new business models for journalism. He received his PhD in environmental science, policy, and management from the University of California, Berkeley, and his BA degree in environmental studies, English, and biology from St. Olaf College.

West Ham’s £5m Winger Has Gone From Match-Winner To Liability

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Whilst most concentrated on Mads Hermansen’s inability to make saves during West Ham’s 2-1 defeat to Blackburn Rovers, it allowed somebody else to escape the glare of criticism. That player was Manor Solomon, because, to put it frankly, he was awful when coming on as a substitute to replace Edvin Austbø at Ewood Park.

The Israeli international has been shocking for West Ham recently, ironically since the last round of international fixtures in September and October, when he represented his country four times and returned to East London looking completely knackered. His performances have deteriorated to the point where I struggle to see what he’s currently offering the team.

We now find ourselves in the rather strange situation where, with West Ham entering another international break, we’re hoping Solomon can go away with Israel and somehow regain some form. Because as things stand, he is simply not a viable option in the current West Ham team.

No doubt West Ham manager Scott Parker, being something of a Championship expert, would have had reasonably positive opinions of Solomon, having witnessed him tearing up the division for Leeds United during their promotion season. However, he’ll have been left in no illusion that the version of Solomon he’s inherited since replacing Nuno Espírito Santo is a long way from that player.

Solomon stunk the place out in the first half against Cardiff City before being substituted, and when introduced in place of Austbø against Blackburn, he made the team noticeably worse. It’s particularly frustrating because Austbø had looked lively and dangerous, offering the directness and attacking intent that Solomon currently seems unable to provide.

Nuno Espírito Santo pushed hard for the signing of Manor Solomon

It’s important to remember that, whilst West Ham haven’t spent a fortune bringing him in, Solomon was signed for a reported £5 million precisely because he was supposed to guarantee goals and assists in a Championship team. Unfortunately, that looks a long way off at present, and the attacking contribution we expected simply hasn’t materialised consistently enough.

Solomon has been called up by Israel for two UEFA Nations League fixtures during the November international break. He will face Kosovo away on Saturday 14 November before Israel take on Austria in Debrecen, Hungary, on Tuesday 17 November.

The winger also featured in all four of Israel’s September and October Nations League matches, so another spell of international football is hardly guaranteed to leave him refreshed. Nevertheless, West Ham and Scott Parker will be hoping those two games can kick-start Solomon into something approaching his previous form.

There is clearly a talented Championship player in there, as he demonstrated during his time at Leeds. But the player who was supposed to provide West Ham with goals, assists and attacking quality is currently struggling to make any meaningful impact.

Parker has the international break to work with much of his squad, but Solomon will be away with Israel rather than spending that time on the training pitch with his new manager. Perhaps a change of environment and a couple of international appearances will do him some good, because West Ham desperately need the Manor Solomon who helped Leeds win promotion, not the one we’ve been watching recently.

With over a decade of professional sports media experience, he is a frequent West Ham United analyst and contributor for Sky Sports News, BBC Football Focus, and BBC Radio 5 Live. He previously served as an official video and content creator for West Ham United FC during the club's tenure at Upton Park and featured in the official documentary commemorating the historic final game at the Boleyn Ground.

9 foods in the Mediterranean diet and the trades behind them, per Mayo Clinic

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Mayo Clinic's Mediterranean diet advice works as a set of trades. See what 9 foods replace and how firm the evidence behind each one is.

Most diets are known by what they forbid. The Mediterranean diet makes more sense through what people who follow it eat at almost every meal and what they save for a few nights a week. Produce, whole grains and olive oil are everyday foods in this way of eating, while fish and eggs are among the foods kept for only a few meals each week. How often a food appears is a sign of how much it matters to the pattern, and the pattern sets proportions across a week rather than a fixed menu. That schedule turns most food choices into trades. When a reader moves one food into a daily slot, another food has to leave the plate, and the health case for the change often depends on which food left.

The diet earned its reputation for heart health from people who ate this way as a whole culture, not from people who added one food to an otherwise unchanged diet. The research behind it comes mostly from Mediterranean populations, according to StatPearls. That evidence describes a whole way of eating more than a set of portions. For that reason, health advice on the diet is about starting a pattern of eating, not taking a dose of any single food, and the benefit belongs to the whole pattern before it belongs to any one item. A reader can turn that into a working rule: follow the shape of the diet faithfully, and treat any single serving number as a target to aim near rather than a line that guarantees a result.

Mayo Clinic lays out practical steps for starting a heart-healthy Mediterranean eating pattern, covering foods from seafood and nuts to eggs and herbs and spices. Each of those steps comes down to a trade, and the evidence behind the trades is not equally strong. Some swaps have direct proof behind them. Others are cautious limits set where scientists still disagree. A reader who knows which is which can decide where to be strict and where to be flexible. The slides that follow take each food in turn and look at what it replaces, how strong its case is and how closely a reader needs to follow the amount attached to it.

The vegetable target looks very different on the plate depending on how the vegetables are prepared. One serving of leafy greens is two cups, while one serving of cooked vegetables is half a cup. The same four servings can be a heaped salad bowl or a modest side dish. A reader who struggles to finish large salads can reach the target with smaller portions of cooked vegetables.

Eating produce every day is common ground, but the exact counts are less fixed than the habit. Sharon F. Daley of Cape Cod Hospital in Hyannis, Mass., and Melissa R. Hinson of Conemaugh Memorial describe a guideline framework in StatPearls that differs from Mayo Clinic's daily serving counts for vegetables and fruit. A reader can hold firm to eating produce every day and to fruit in place of dessert, and let the balance between fruit and vegetables follow taste, the season and the budget.

Whole grains belong on the plate every day because their benefit is broad rather than dramatic. Mayo Clinic advises eating them each day, and the research points the same way. A European Heart Journal analysis of over 6,500 adults links whole grains to possible improvement in a group of heart risk factors rather than in just one, according to Harvard Health. Small gains across several risk factors can add up to more than a large change in one. People get that kind of modest benefit only by repeating the habit, so whole grains fit an everyday meal better than an occasional one.

The case for whole grains has limits. The research tracks risk factors, not heart attacks or strokes. It supports whole grains as a sensible staple without showing that they prevent heart disease, which makes them a well-founded bet rather than a settled cure. Mayo Clinic measures whole grains by the ounce, and 1 ounce is about one slice of bread. A single slice of whole-wheat toast at breakfast therefore counts as only one serving. A reader who stops there keeps the daily habit in name more than on the plate.

Eating more than a slice or two is easier when bread is not the only source. Mayo Clinic also names bulgur, barley and farro, which a cook can use as the base of a salad, a grain bowl or a side dish. With those grains on hand, a large daily helping becomes part of an ordinary meal rather than a chore.

Make whole grains the base of the plate at every meal, and count each slice of bread as just one ounce toward the day.

Cooks get the heart benefit of olive oil only when they use it in place of butter. The oil has a reputation as a health food in its own right, but Mayo Clinic includes unsaturated plant fats in the diet because people who use them instead of butter-type fats cut their heart disease risk and their LDL cholesterol. The gain depends on the fat that leaves the pan more than on the oil that goes in. A cook who adds olive oil to salads but still cooks with butter has added a food without making the trade.

The proven payoff of the swap is narrow, and it centers on bad cholesterol. Fats from plants that are unsaturated appear to bring down a person's LDL cholesterol along with total cholesterol. Replacing butter with plant oils and spreads had no effect on HDL cholesterol or triglycerides, according to USDA NESR. The improvement is real, but it touches one part of the blood lipid picture rather than all of it.

The heart-disease benefit rests on softer ground. "Replacing saturated fats with unsaturated fats may help lower the risk of heart disease," Mayo Clinic staff wrote. Research on trading butter for plant oil does not settle the question, USDA NESR reports, and the studies differ on whether the swap raises or lowers cardiovascular risk and by how much. People can count on the swap to improve their cholesterol and should treat any heart protection beyond that as a likely bonus rather than a promise.

Some plant fats belong on the butter side of the trade. Blood pressure stayed the same when palm oil, coconut oil or cocoa butter gave way to unsaturated oils, according to USDA NESR. These tropical fats still count among the ones to swap out, though a reader should not expect lower blood pressure from the change. At the store, the useful test is whether a fat is unsaturated, as olive oil is, not whether it comes from a plant.

Make liquid plant oil the everyday cooking fat in place of butter, and count it as a cholesterol step rather than a cure for heart disease.

Fish is one of the proteins the diet saves for a few nights a week, and part of its value lies in the meat it replaces on those nights. The health case for the fish itself is firmer for some benefits than for others. Mayo Clinic says the omega-3 fats in seafood may curb inflammation and stroke risk, and the NIH Office of Dietary Supplements reports that intake of EPA and DHA, two omega-3 fats, lowers triglyceride levels. The triglyceride effect is stated as established, while the stroke and inflammation benefits that justify eating fish are worded only as possibilities. Mayo Clinic links the omega-3 fats to fatty fish, yet no recommended amounts have been established for EPA and DHA. Omega-3 is a plausible reason to eat fish, not a measured prescription, so the safer bet is the fish itself, eaten as a meal.

Mayo Clinic asks for more fish than the heart association does, setting a target of 2 to 3 seafood meals a week. The American Heart Association calls for 1 to 2 servings weekly, according to the NIH Office of Dietary Supplements. Either number makes sense only as a swap. A fish dinner helps when it takes the place of a meat dinner, not when someone adds it to an otherwise unchanged week. Each fish night also calls for only a modest portion, since an adult serving is 3 to 5 ounces. A reader who reliably eats fish instead of meat at the heart association's lower level is already making the trade, and Mayo Clinic's range is the level to aim toward.

Fish oil does not stand in for fish. The diet ties the benefit to fatty fish. Unless a person's cardiovascular risk is high, the American Heart Association doesn't advise taking omega-3 pills, according to the NIH Office of Dietary Supplements. A person who takes a capsule still eats the meat dinner, so the main part of the trade never happens. The cooking method matters too. "Stay away from deep-fried fish," Mayo Clinic staff said. Frying a fillet turns a fish night back into the kind of meal the pattern is meant to replace.

With seafood, the number is negotiable but the swap is not: grilled or baked fish on a night that would have been meat is the habit that counts.

Nuts belong in the Mediterranean diet as a backup source of fat, not the main one. Extra virgin olive oil does most of the work of supplying unsaturated fat at every meal, and nuts rank behind it as a supplier of that same fat. That supporting role keeps the portion small. The diet calls for nuts in four quarter-cup servings a week, according to Mayo Clinic. A quarter cup is a small handful, so each serving adds a modest amount to the fat a cook already gets from the oil in the pan. A reader who sprinkles almonds on a salad or eats a handful of walnuts in the afternoon is adding to the fat the oil already supplies, not building meals around nuts. Nuts matter here for the healthy fat they contribute to the whole pattern, more than for any food they replace.

The advice also covers a specific kind of nut, not every product that contains them. Only raw, unsalted nuts count toward the weekly target, so a can of salted cashews or a seasoned snack mix doesn't meet it as written. That gives the reader a clear way to divide the advice. The weekly count works as a target to aim near, much like the other serving numbers in the diet. The choice at the store is a firm requirement, and it is the one part of this advice that the reader controls completely every time they shop.

Nuts are the diet's measured extra: a small weekly portion that counts only when it is bought plain.

Mayo Clinic advises eating dairy only in moderate portions and choosing low-fat versions over higher-fat ones. The trade therefore happens inside the dairy case, with leaner products going in and richer ones coming out. For most people, the change would show up at breakfast, since whole milk and 2% milk are the products the advice asks them to cut back. The call for moderate portions and the call for low-fat products do not rest on equally firm proof, and a reader should know which one to hold tightly.

Recent research has called the low-fat rule into question. Participants in a recent trial ate full-fat dairy three times a day, according to the University of Toronto, and their results did not show the harm the low-fat rule is meant to prevent. "Those that had three servings of dairy didn't have adverse levels of blood cholesterol or lipids or evidence of insulin resistance," said Harvey Anderson, a professor of nutritional sciences at the Temerty Faculty of Medicine at the University of Toronto. Blood pressure also improved in the groups eating dairy, so on that measure the full-fat eaters did better rather than merely no worse. For these markers, choosing low-fat looks more like a precaution against a harm the trial did not find than a proven requirement.

That challenge still leaves moderation standing. The trial lasted only 12 weeks, according to the University of Toronto. That is long enough to show early changes in cholesterol and blood pressure but too short to show what years of full-fat dairy would do. Even the researcher questioning the low-fat rule does not argue for more dairy, since Anderson favors eating many different foods and limiting how much of any one goes on the plate. His view and Mayo Clinic's call for moderate portions point the same way, and the trial's three daily servings were an amount researchers tested, not a license.

Moderation is the rule worth keeping, though Mayo Clinic gives no daily or weekly count for it. Its one concrete gauge is the size of a single serving, which matches four dice, and readers have to judge for themselves how many of those servings still count as moderate.

Keep dairy portions modest even without a set number, and treat low-fat as a sensible default that researchers have reopened for debate, not as a rule to obey at all costs.

The Mediterranean diet asks people to eat less red and processed meat, but it stops short of banning it. Mayo Clinic advises reducing both, and the heart benefit of doing so depends on what takes the meat's place. Among people who ate pulses instead of all their red meat, the risk of ischemic heart disease was 10% lower, according to EMJ. The gain belongs to the trade, not to the cut alone. A plate with the meat simply removed does only half the job, and the empty space needs beans, lentils or other pulses.

The limit worth holding firmly is the one on processed meat. EMJ reports that ischemic heart disease risk ran 15% lower when pulses took the place of processed meat, and cardiovascular disease risk ran 13% lower when they stood in for processed poultry. Both results involve processed foods, whether red meat or poultry, so the common thread is processing rather than the color of the meat. These findings center on processed meat and say nothing on their own about whether fresh cuts carry the same risk. That fits the diet's own guidance, which still allows lean cuts in small portions.

The findings come from a large group of adults in the U.K., 118,272 people in the UK Biobank, according to EMJ. A link of this kind supports the trade, but it doesn't prove that eating pulses instead of processed meat is what lowers the risk.

The practical rule is to be strict about processed meat and to fill the gap with pulses. Lean fresh cuts in small portions are an allowance the diet makes on purpose, not a lapse to feel guilty about.

Eggs sit on the limited side of the Mediterranean diet, and the limit falls on the yolk rather than on the egg as a whole. Mayo Clinic allows up to four whole eggs a week, and it sets that limit because of the saturated fat in the yolk rather than because of any doubt about the egg's nutritional value. For most people the cap asks for little change, since typical intake already runs three to four eggs a week, according to TIME. A reader who follows it mainly avoids drifting toward an omelet every morning.

The heart risk behind the cap is real but small for any one person. In 2019, researchers estimated that an extra half egg a day would mean about 11 additional heart disease cases among every 1,000 people across 30 years, according to TIME. Nearly everyone in that group would see no difference, which makes this an effect that appears across a population rather than a sharp danger to each eater. The four-egg figure is a nutritionists' suggestion rather than a number taken from a study, and the science underneath it is open. "The existing literature is still controversial and inconclusive for nutrition experts and researchers to conclude the safety of eggs," said Victor Wenze Zhong, a postdoctoral fellow at Northwestern University's Feinberg School of Medicine and a co-author of the study behind that estimate. A fifth egg in one week breaks no rule. A steady climb past four, though, moves toward the intake the research flags.

The most useful trade happens inside the egg itself. Mayo Clinic's cap counts whole eggs and targets the fat in the yolk, while egg whites contain no cholesterol, according to TIME. Zhong himself recommends whites as a stand-in for whole eggs. A scramble made with one yolk and several extra whites keeps eggs on the plate without adding to the weekly yolk count.

Be strict about how many yolks you eat and relaxed about how many eggs.

Herbs and spices earn their place mainly for what they let a cook leave out of a dish. Mayo Clinic lists them among the foods the Mediterranean diet is most often high in, which places seasoning among the diet's regular foods rather than its occasional ones. The diet calls for them because a cook who seasons food richly needs less salt. The CDC names spices, garlic and citrus juice as substitutes for salt in cooking, so the swap is shared health advice rather than one diet's quirk. A cook who keeps those on hand has several familiar ways to make food taste full without the shaker.

The swap does less than it seems to, because the saltshaker was never where most of the salt came from. Packaged foods and restaurant meals supply most of the sodium people eat, the CDC reports, and bread adds more because people eat it often even though each serving carries only moderate sodium. Seasoning at home therefore trims only a minor share of a person's salt. Bread is also one of the foods the diet encourages, so a cook who seasons generously still takes in salt that no herb can remove. The full trade has two parts: season richly at home, and cut back on packaged foods, sauces and takeout.

Mayo Clinic sets no serving target for herbs and spices, so a reader has no amount to measure against. That fits the trade itself, because success shows in the salt left out rather than in the amount of seasoning put in.

Here a reader can be as generous as they like, and the strictness belongs to the salt hidden in the rest of their food.

Just Turn the Heat On? That Might Be Why Your Skin Suddenly Feels So Dry

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

A few weeks ago, I rented a big cabin outside of the city with friends. It was the first cold weekend in New York, the kind where you might actually need to throw on the heavy jackets that were too much for those 55-degree fall days. Nestled in this mountain house, we cozied up around a fire in fleece sweatshirts and fuzzy blankets. Before bed, we turned the heat on. I slept cozy like a baby in the womb, but immediately upon waking, my skin felt tight and dry. I coated myself in moisturizer, but it was no use. My face—which is usually oily and acne-prone—was dull and rough, like it’d never produced a drop of oil in its 29 years.

At first, I thought it was a sign of the times. I’m about to turn 30, and my skin’s just getting drier. But when I headed back to Brooklyn, where my stuffy apartment produces enough natural heat on its own to not need the radiator cranked up, my skin went back to normal.

I’ve heard of this phenomenon before—when your skin gets dehydrated once you turn on the heat in your house—but this was my first experience. And I was determined to get to the bottom of it before my radiator goes on for good and the winter uglies take over. Two dermatologists weighed in—and it looks like this isn’t necessarily preventable but totally treatable.

“Indoor heating lowers the relative humidity in the air, which makes moisture evaporate from your skin more quickly,” says dermatologist Annette Czernik, MD. “Our skin naturally produces oils that help protect it and keep moisture in, but sometimes that's not enough when the air is really dry.” You might apply moisturizer and feel like you didn’t even do anything, she adds.

You’re also not always in the clear if you have oily skin, says Dr. Czernik. “A lot of people don't realize that your skin can be oily and dehydrated at the same time,” she explains. “This is because your skin can still produce oil while losing moisture from the dry air.”

You may also feel tightness, roughness, stinging, flaking, and even itching. “As your skin loses more moisture, its protective barrier can become weaker,” says Dr. Czernik, hence why you can experience some irritation as your skin isn’t as strong enough to fight it off. The air can also make your skin look dull or even feel more sensitive, since it’s working so hard to hold onto the moisture it needs, explains Dr. Czernik. “In more severe cases, you might experience redness, peeling, or even small cracks in your skin,” she adds.

Naturally, with colder air outside, your skin might get drier too, only exacerbating the issue. “When the temperature drops outside, the air usually holds less moisture,” says Dr. Czernik. “Then, when you go indoors, you're exposed to heated air that can also be very dry.” You’re basically losing moisture on both sides, no matter what you do. “Going back and forth between the cold outdoors and warm indoors can make that dryness more noticeable,” she adds. “This is why someone might have perfectly normal skin during the summer but struggle with dryness all winter.”

“When you turn the heat on, the air inside your home can become very dry,” explains dermatologist Ariel Ostad, MD. And one of the easiest ways to add moisture back into the air is a humidifier. “This can help slow down how quickly your skin loses water throughout the day or night,” Dr. Ostad adds.

He recommends getting a bedside humidifier, like the Canopy Bedside Humidifier, for your bedroom and running it while you’re sleeping. “You may notice that your skin feels less tight and dry when you wake up in the morning,” he says. “It can also help with dry lips and irritated skin.” But remember to clean it regularly, as bacteria and mold can build up in your device.

“If your skin starts getting drier, I would first look at the products you're using every day,” says Dr. Ostad. Both Dr. Ostad and Dr. Czernik recommend switching to a thicker moisturizer. Ideally, find one that both moisturizes (meaning it contains ingredients that pull water into your skin, like squalane, hyaluronic acid, panthenol, and glycerin) and seals in hydration (aka ingredients that act as a physical barrier to reduce moisture loss, including ceramides, oils, butters, and silicones). But you could also opt for two moisturizers nightly. I prefer to use a lightweight moisturizer, then follow it up with a barrier cream.

“It's also a good idea to apply your moisturizer right after washing your face or getting out of the shower,” adds Dr. Czernik. If your skin is still damp, even better. This helps pull all that water into your skin (which helps plump it up and decrease dullness), plus it eliminates time for moisture to evaporate from your skin.

Just as hot air can pull water from your skin, so can hot water, explains Dr. Czernik. It doesn’t mean you need to do a daily cold plunge, but opt for mild to warm water temperatures, as scalding water can also increase skin irritation.

Some of the products in your routine could actually be making your dryness worse. Put down all the strong exfoliants, whether it’s your salicylic acid cleanser, an acid-based peel pad, or a retinol serum.

Instead, Dr. Ostad suggests using a gentle, fragrance-free cleanser “that won't strip your skin of its natural oils.” And if you love your exfoliants, you don’t have to ditch them completely, he adds, but you might want to cut back to once or twice per week at most. “You don't need to completely change your skincare routine, but making a few small adjustments can really help during the colder months,” he says.

“If your skin is just a little dry, you can usually manage it at home with a good moisturizer,” says Dr. Ostad. “However, if the dryness continues for a few weeks despite changing your skincare routine, I would recommend seeing a dermatologist.” If you notice your skin becomes extremely itchy or irritated, that’s a good sign to see a doctor too. “Cracking, bleeding, or painful peeling are signs that something more serious could be going on,” adds Dr. Ostad.

Beth Gillette is the beauty editor at Cosmopolitan with eight years of experience researching, writing, and editing skin, hair, makeup, nail, and fragrance stories.

The 7 Best Hiking Boots for Weak Ankles in 2026

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

If you buy through our links, we may earn an affiliate commission. This supports our mission to get more people active and outside.Learn about Outside Online's affiliate link policy

Hiking boots have lost some ground to trail runners and low-cut shoes over the past decade, but don’t write them off just yet: If you have weak ankles or are prone to stumbles, an old-school hiking boot may be exactly what you need, especially if your trips often involve rough terrain or heavier loads. Besides providing protection from rolls and twists, a mid-cut boot can help lower your injury risk by supporting your foot and ankle and easing the fatigue and soreness that builds up over the course of a long backpacking trip.

We’ve gone through our favorite hiking boots of 2026 and picked our seven faves for hikers with problem ankles, four from our men’s list and three from our women’s roundup, both curated by Blair Braverman, the expert reviewer at our sibling site Backpacker. Our picks run the gamut from super-supportive leather lace-ups to lightweight mid-cuts with a sneaker-like feel. All earned our testers’ approval over hundreds of trail miles, so you can confidently buy the best hiking boot for your weak ankles.

Weight: 16.9 oz (men’s) / 13.4 oz (women’s) Sizes: Men’s 37-47.5 (EU) / Women’s 36-42 (EU)

Pros and Cons + Out-of-box comfort + Versatility + Support – On the pricey side

If you want the best hiking boot for weak ankles that money can buy this year—no matter how much money it costs—this is it. While it’s designed for three-season alpine use, we found it a perfect tool for rugged backpacking trips, thanks to a durable PU midsole with a polypropylene stiffener, a “bouncy and cushioned” PU-injected rubber outsole, and a reinforced toe and heel that looked “pristine” after more than 100 often-rough miles on the trail. Read the full review.

Weight: 20.1 oz (men’s) / 16.6 oz (women’s) Sizes: Men’s 7-15 / Women’s 5-12

Pros and Cons + Excellent waterproofing + Durable – Some break-in required – Heavy

If you love an old-school hiker, the Keen Targhee IV Waterproof Mid is the hiking boot for you. This generously cushioned leather mid-cut shrugged off scrapes from rocks and comes with a lifetime guarantee against delamination of the sole. Its bulletproof waterproofing made it perfectly suited for wet, sloppy trail. Read the full review.

Weight: 12 oz (men’s) / 11 oz (women’s) Sizes: Men’s 7-13 / Women’s 5-10

Pros and Cons + Lightweight + Excellent grip + Responsive – Firm cushioning

One pound on your feet burns as much energy as three pounds in your pack, so we’d call this lightweight mid-cut hiking boot a calorie-saver. The Arc’teryx Kopec Mid GTX is a perfect do-anything boot, as comfortable on high-tempo day hikes as it is toting a pack with a week of supplies, but still wards off foot and ankle fatigue with its supportive midsole. Read the full review.

Weight: 14.5 oz (men’s) / 11.5 oz (women’s) Sizes: Men’s 7-14 / Women’s 5-11

Pros and Cons + Stylish + Supportive + Cushy and responsive ride – Break-in required

Sometimes, your pack is just going to be heavy—think supply-intensive deep wilderness trips or family outings where you end up carrying other people’s gear. The Danner N45 Mid GTX hiking boot feels tailor-made for those missions, especially if you have weak ankles, with bouncy nitrogen-infused foam in the midsole, a TPU rock plate, and a breathable nylon-poly upper. It doesn’t hurt that its black-on-black design looks sharp, too. Read the full review.

Weight: 17.6 oz (men’s) / 15.9 oz (women’s) Sizes: Men’s 7-14 / Women’s 6-11

Pros and Cons + Out-of-box comfort + Plush ankle support – Too warm for hot weather

“Old-school substance without the old-school break-in period” is how our reviewer described The North Face Offtrail Hike LT Mid GTX. An EVA midsole paired with a flexible rockguard provided cushion underfoot, while a well-padded upper and heel cup kept the foot comfortable. The result? A supportive hiking boot for weak ankles that our testers said felt like “a firm hug” even when the trail got rough. Read the full review.

Pros and Cons + Durable + Very supportive + Out-of-box comfort – Too stiff for day hikes – Expensive

The Meindl Air Revolution Alpine 4.1 isn’t for everyone: With its stiff sole, heavy weight, and aggressive tread, this mountaineering-adjacent hiking boot is best for rough terrain, heavy loads, and long stretches above treeline. But we were shocked by the out-of-box comfort and extreme breathability, too. Read the full review.

Weight: 20.8 oz (men’s) / 20.5 oz (women’s) Sizes: Men’s 7-13 / Women’s 5-10

Pros and Cons + Durable + Good-looking + Ankle support for heavy packs – Tedious to lace properly

Think of the Arc’teryx Konseal Trek as James Bond’s car: Its slick looks conceal “an arsenal of protection,” as our reviewer noted. Among its tricks: a bonded Gore-Tex membrane that provides waterproofing while keeping the boot slim; a mid-height, lightly-padded cuff outfitted with a Kevlar exterior for durability even on talus-strewn slopes; and a carbon fiber shank worked into the EVA midsole, so your ride is both comfortable and stable. Read the full review.

First, look at the cuff: You want an upper that’s stiff enough to support your joint if you stumble or step on a loose rock, but not so stiff that you can’t maintain a natural stride on the tail. Which of those you prioritize depends on your objectives: Long days on relatively smooth trail call for softer support, while stiffer boots may help prevent injury on steep, loose ascents. Pay attention to the padding on the cuff, which should cradle your ankle rather than squeeze it.

Midsole construction can also make a big difference in how a boot feels on the trail: An EVA midsole will feel bouncier and softer, more akin to a running shoe, while a PU midsole will provide a somewhat stiffer, more supportive ride, though often at a weight penalty. A shank, often made of a material like carbon fiber, will provide additional stiffness and structural support to the sole, though it should still allow your foot to flex naturally.

Finally, pay attention to the outsole; while it may not play a role in supporting your ankle directly, a grippy tread can help prevent stumbles and slips, which can lead to injuries. Softer rubber will grip better on hard surfaces, though it will shorten the boot’s lifespan as it wears away faster. A harder, less pliable rubber may feel more slippery on surfaces like slickrock, but can grip well on soft trail, allowing its tread to “bite” into the path.

Mostly we just let our testers do what they do best: walk. We called in dozens of different hiking boots and shoes and handed them out to 15 testers with instructions to put on some serious miles. They took short hikes and 30-mile backpacking trips, on-trail and off. They scrambled up peaks and down canyons, logging more than 700 miles in total. As they trekked along, we asked them to consider everything from fit to grip, cushioning to stability.

Because pack weight can influence all these variables, we had them hike with both light loads and heavy packs. And to understand how boots and shoes performed in different weather conditions and environments, we spread testers and samples across the continent, from coastal British Columbia to New England’s windy summits.

After at least two testers have tried every shoe, we have them fill out a detailed questionnaire. Then we send them back out with more shoes. The process is ongoing and endless. With every review we compare the results to all the other hikers we’ve tested over the years. Because ultimately we’re not just looking for the best new hiking boots for weak ankles, we want the best hiking boots for weak ankles, period.

Category manager Wisconsin-based Blair Braverman is a long-distance dogsledder who loves hiking whenever she’s not mushing. Her longest backpacking trip was the 400-mile Oregon Coast Trail, and her favorite is Colorado’s Four-Pass Loop.

‘The end of Tesla!’: Cybertruck diehards turn on Elon Musk after he pulls the plug

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Tesla’s polarizing pickup is heading to the scrap heap, leaving its biggest online boosters feeling betrayed and questioning Musk’s next big pivot.

Tesla’s polarizing pickup is heading to the scrap heap, leaving its biggest online boosters feeling betrayed and questioning Musk’s next big pivot.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The Cybertruck is heading to that shiny cyber-dumpster in the sky, leaving the folks who built online identities around Elon Musk’s polarizing pickup feeling bereft. Usually, Musk’s fanbase treads lightly around their idol’s mercurial decisions. This time, however, the truck’s demise has sparked rare backlash within the Tesla community — aimed squarely at the guy who signed its death warrant.

Troy Meekhof, who posts on numerous social platforms under the moniker “The Cybertruck Guy,” has been vocal about his disappointment in Musk’s decision to wind down Cybertruck production by the end of summer 2027. (The original plan was to start winding down production in the spring, but then Musk decided to extend it a few months.)

In a 33-minute video on YouTube, Meekhof argues that Tesla never fully committed to making the Cybertruck a success, citing a lack of advertising, confusing pricing changes, and the decision to shift focus before the truck could fully establish itself in the market.

“It feels like they didn’t try to make Cybertruck a success,” he said.

He also had choice words for Musk’s reasons for ending the Cybertruck’s short run to make room at Giga Texas for the fully autonomous Cybercab: “Cancelling all of your best products... in favor of an autonomous platform that remains both software and regulatory constrained... seems like an absolutely terrible idea,” Meekhof vented.

The Cybertruck wasn’t the only Tesla product to meet an untimely end — at least according to those with stakes (and often stock options) in the company’s success. Earlier this year, Tesla discontinued the Model S and Model X, two of its flagship vehicles. With the end of the Cybertruck, Tesla is now left with just the Model 3 and Model Y as vehicles you can actually buy. (The Semi is strictly a commercial vehicle, while the Cybercab’s availability as a consumer product remains dubious, at best.)

“Cancelling Cybertruck after all the years of getting it to production to ‘make room for CyberCab’ is a lame excuse,” @TeslaXplored wrote on X. “There are like 500 CyberCabs in one city that only Tesla influencers are riding in. Taking away consumer cars instead of building what the people want will be the end of Tesla!”

He added, “Very sad what happened to Tesla over the last 5 years.”

Meanwhile, at least one prominent aftermarket company is offering to step in and pick up the mantle. Unplugged Performance, which makes aftermarket accessories, said it would produce the Cybertruck and other vehicles on the truck platform under license, with investors funding the effort: “Worth a conversation?”

Musk, for his part, seems less interested in litigating the particulars of Cybertruck production and more intent on selling a vision of Tesla’s future centered around the Cybercab and humanoid robots. “Cybercab will do a majority of all passenger miles in any jurisdiction where it is not prohibited by law,” Musk wrote on X earlier this week. “Obvious to anyone with eyes and a brain.”

He also pushed back on claims that the Cybertruck was killed because it was unprofitable. “The Cybertruck order rate far exceeds our production rate,” Musk said. “We will try to make as many as possible before shutting down the line in about 8 months, but will have to return deposits for a lot of people.”

To be sure, Cybertruck sales have been disappointing over its entire production run. Musk initially theorized that Tesla could sell 250,000-500,000 Cybertrucks annually, but the actual numbers have been far below those projections. Last year, Tesla is estimated to have sold around 20,000 trucks; the company is on track to sell fewer than that this year.

Whether Musk’s autonomous utopia pans out or ends up joining the Cybertruck in the vaporware graveyard remains to be seen. For now, the stainless-steel wedge that was supposed to be the ultimate symbol of an indestructible future has become a monument to Musk’s shifting attention span — and a stark reminder to Tesla’s most loyal boosters that in Elon’s world, nothing should be taken for granted.
